# PREFACE.

<image-desc>A decorative headpiece consisting of two rows of stylized floral or leaf-like ornaments. Below the title, a large ornate woodcut initial 'A' entwined with vine and leaf motifs.</image-desc>

**A**my Reader, just as I have no doubt that the story of Polydamas <note>A famous Greek athlete from the 5th century BCE, known for his immense strength; he died attempting to hold up the roof of a collapsing cave.</note> is known to you—who, wishing to stop a heavy boulder rolling from the top of a mountain, was crushed beneath it—so too am I quite sure that you will not fail to apply this example to my own purpose, in order to judge the risk and the difficulty of this undertaking of mine. This task might seem even more perilous to you if you had seen, as I have, how deeply these common opinions—which I undertake to combat and overthrow—are rooted in the imagination <note>original: "fantaisie"; in this period, it refers to the faculty of the mind that forms images, often associated with delusion.</note> of certain historians, and obstinately maintained by the majority of our demonographers <term>Demonographers: writers or "experts" who documented the nature and hierarchy of demons and the practice of witchcraft</term>. These men, not being of a sufficiently strong and well-tempered constitution <note>original: "complexion"; the early modern medical theory that a person's physical and mental health was determined by the balance of bodily fluids or "humors."</note>...

<summary>The author introduces his work by comparing his challenge against established beliefs in demonology to the dangerous strength of Polydamas, warning of the difficulty in uprooting deeply held superstitions.</summary>
<keywords>Polydamas, demonology, skepticism, historians, medical humors, intellectual history</keywords>

# PREFACE.

of discretion and resistance to the squalls and storms of common and erroneous opinions. This is why, in order to omit nothing that you might desire for your enlightenment, it is only necessary to deduce and explain two words in good faith, and this with the brevity that is required for a Preface.

The first of these will notify you and perhaps make you marvel at the fact that I have taken the occasion to compose such a laborious <term>Apology<note>A formal written defense of a person or idea, from the Greek 'apologia'.</note></term> over an encounter of almost no consequence. You know, as I believe, that toward the end of last <term>Lent<note>original: "Caresme"; the forty-day period of fasting and penitence before Easter.</note></term>, a small book was published entitled, *New judgment of what has been said and written for and against the book of the Curious Doctrine of the wits of this time*: at the end of which, he who was the Author had inserted two very short and succinct invectives against Homer and Virgil. For what purpose and with how little reason, this is not the place to discourse upon; but it remains that in the one regarding Virgil, he accuses him of having been a notorious Enchanter and <term>Necromancer<note>original: "Necromantien"; one who practices magic involving communication with the dead.</note></term>, and of having done an infinity of marvelous things by means of his Magic. # PREFACE.

immediately acknowledged to have been transcribed word for word from the latest book that Mr. de Lancre <note>Pierre de Lancre (1553–1631), a French judge and demonologist famous for his brutal witch trials in Labourd.</note> had printed against the disbelief in Sorcery <term>Sortilege: the practice of magic or divination, specifically associated with witchcraft</term>. From this, reflecting upon what I had read, and remembering that not only Virgil <note>The Roman poet Virgil was often reimagined in medieval and Renaissance folklore as a powerful sorcerer.</note> but almost all great personages were similarly suspected of Magic, I immediately began to doubt that this was done rightly or with reason. Having cleared up many difficulties for myself which prevented me from reaching a full understanding of this truth, I did not wish to be so little devoted to the public good, and to the memory of all these famous personages, as to deny the communication of these proofs justifying their innocence to those who do not, and perhaps will not soon have, the time or convenience to research them with as much care and diligence as I have forced myself to do in this Apology <term>Apologie: a formal written defense of one's opinions or conduct</term>. This work presents to you at the very outset the assured means and necessary conditions for judging Authors, and principally Historians and Demonographers <note>Writers who specialized in the study of demons and the occult</note>, who are the two primary architects of this labyrinth of false opinions, from which it would be great-

# PREFACE.

It is extremely difficult to extricate oneself without the direction and guidance of this thread <note>A reference to Ariadne’s thread, used to navigate the labyrinth of historical accusations.</note>, of which I have chosen for this occasion to attach one end to this first Chapter. Immediately following this, I have placed the chapter on Magic and its various types, so that no one can remain ignorant of the chief and principal point of the accusation and the defense. This consists in the distinction between <term>Diabolical Magic</term> <note>original: "Magie Diabolique"; sorcery involving pacts with demons.</note> and <term>Natural Magic</term> <note>original: "Magie Naturelle"; the study of the hidden powers of nature and science.</note>. Following that, I have researched the general causes that may have led to this suspicion—namely, Politics, profound and extraordinary doctrine, the knowledge of <term>Mathematics</term> <note>In this period, "mathematics" often included astronomy and astrology, which were frequently confused with sorcery.</note>, the composition of books, superstitious observations, heresy, hatred, the ignorance of the age, the excessive readiness to believe many fabulous things, and the lack of care and judgment among Authors and Writers. All of these are reduced and explained within five Chapters, which have opened and smoothed the path for me to undertake, in the fourteen that follow, the specific defense of Zoroaster, Orpheus, Pythagoras, Democritus, and others, both ancient and Modern. In this, I have not followed the order of the time in which they flourished, because it

### PREFACE.

It seemed more appropriate to me to arrange them under the titles of their various dignities and offices, so that having done so for the Philosophers, Physicians, Monks, Bishops, Popes, and all the other famous personages whom I had proposed to defend; it only remained for me to attach the other end of my net to the final Chapter of this <term>Apology<note>A formal written defense of one's opinions or conduct, rather than an expression of regret.</note></term>, which will show you in conclusion by what means all these falsehoods are maintained, and what one must expect from them if they are not repressed.

Now, just as this first word only tends to declare myself and make my intention known; so must it be admitted that the point I now wish to deduce has no other goal than to excuse, or rather justify, myself for having variegated my French with some Latin sentences and authorities: For I know well that many writers who are esteemed among the most polished of this century can only look with a disdainful eye upon the writings of those who do not make a profession, as they do, of composing fables and amorous encounters for the entertainment of women and small children. But as I am grateful to them

# PREFACE.

willing to proportion their style to the capacity of those to whom they write; likewise, they should not take it ill if I do the same; and if I have governed myself by this consideration, in order to dress these Latin passages in the French fashion <note>original: "n'habiller à la Françoise ces passages Latins"; the author is explaining his decision to translate Latin citations into the vernacular for his readers</note>, since they have no need to be understood by the common people <term>populace: the general public, often used here to distinguish between scholars and unlearned readers</term>, who are accustomed—when it comes to seeking the truth behind all these calumnies and false suspicions—to rely on the authority of Historians, Demonographers <term>Demonographers: writers who study or document the nature and hierarchy of demons and spirits</term>, and Authors of credit; who maintain the public in these delusions by their own agreement. And in truth, if everyone wished to follow the ardor of those spirits who would rather see a languid and skeletal period <note>a "period" refers to a complex, formal sentence structure</note> in their books than the name or authority of the Authors at whose expense they often compose them; what occasion would remain for us to labor for posterity? # PREFACE.

...ual? Certainly it seems to me that it belongs only to those who never hope to be cited themselves, to cite no one else; and it is too great an ambition to persuade oneself of having ideas capable of satisfying such a great diversity of readers without borrowing anything from others. For if there ever were authors who could truly consider themselves as such, they were, without controversy, Plutarch, Seneca, and Montaigne <note>Michel de Montaigne (1533–1592), the famous French essayist known for his extensive use of classical quotations.</note>, who nevertheless left nothing behind in the works of others that could serve to embellish their own discourses. Witness the Greek and Latin verses <note>original: "vers Grecs & Latins"</note> which are found on almost every line of their works; and among others, that *Consolation* of seven or eight pages which the former <note>Plutarch</note> sent to Apollonius, in which one can count more than one hundred and fifty lines from Homer, and almost as many from Hesiod, Pindar, Sophocles, and Euripides. And furthermore, I do not believe that these new censors of writing styles are so lacking in judgment as to oppose the preceding authorities with that of Epicurus, who, in the three hundred volumes he left behind, had not placed or inserted a single citation,

# PREFACE.

<margin>investigation</margin> because that would provide me with the means of their condemnation, seeing that the works of **Plutarch**, **Seneca**, and **Montaigne** are read, leafed through, sold, and reprinted every day, whereas the catalog of those by **Epicurus** <note>Epicurus (341–270 BCE) was an ancient Greek philosopher; his original writings are mostly lost, known primarily through later summaries.</note> has barely remained for us in the writings of **Diogenes Laertius** <note>A biographer of the Greek philosophers.</note>. I do not say this, however, to approve of the manner of those who voluntarily strip themselves of the riches of their own minds to beg for those of others, who appear only under the brilliance of a borrowed display, and who cover themselves in the armor of others to the point of not even showing the tips of their fingers. But it must be confessed that I am so disgusted by these long and useless discourses given to us nowadays—which the wise **Phocion** <note>An Athenian statesman and general known for his brevity and biting wit.</note> could better than ever compare to a forest of **Cypress trees**, where the trees are beautiful and green, yet nevertheless produce no fruit of value—that I believe those who strike the right balance and hold the middle ground between these two extremes are those who marry their own conceptions with those of the **Ancients**, whenever the subject matter permits it.

<summary>The author defends the practice of citing classical authorities while criticizing those who rely solely on borrowed wisdom or produce flowery but empty prose. He advocates for a balanced style that integrates original thought with ancient wisdom.</summary>
<keywords>literary theory, classical education, Plutarch, Seneca, Montaigne, Epicurus, Phocion, rhetoric, intellectual property</keywords>

# PREFACE.

...so as not to make their readers resemble those in the Prophet Jeremiah <note>A reference to Jeremiah 14:3, describing a drought where people find no water in the cisterns.</note> who, having come to draw water, returned empty-handed, all confused and afflicted. And just as it belongs only to elevated, transcendent souls—those who possess something above the common sort—to give us their conceptions pure, naked, alone, and with no other escort than the truth; and just as it is a mark of a low and debased spirit to undertake nothing of one's own; so too is it the true character of one who is as far removed from vainglory as from ignorance and stupidity, to follow the track and the path beaten by the most learned and sensible men. Such a person does not amuse himself so much with what might trick or tickle the ears of the Readers that he comes to neglect what is necessary for the full and entire satisfaction of their minds. This is what I have particularly endeavored to do in this Apology <term>Apology: a formal written defense of a belief or doctrine, rather than an expression of regret</term>, of which, if you wish to judge while being disinterested from passion and with all sincerity, I assure myself and promise myself so much from your benevolence, that you will not wish to deny it what it has always hoped for: and this principally when you have considered

By the Grace and Privilege of the King <note>A legal document granting exclusive rights to publish a specific work, similar to a modern copyright.</note> given at Paris on the twenty-seventh of March, 1669. By the King in his Council, signed LABORIE. Permission is granted to FRANÇOIS ESCHART, Merchant Bookseller in Paris, to print or cause to be printed a Book entitled, *Apology for the Great Men who have been accused of Magic*, composed by the Lord Naudé <note>Gabriel Naudé (1600–1653), a famous librarian and scholar who sought to debunk myths about historical figures being sorcerers.</note>. And prohibitions are made to all Printers, Booksellers, and other persons, of whatever quality and condition they may be, from printing or causing to be printed the said Book, without the consent of the said Petitioner, or of those who shall have rights from him, during the time and space of five full and completed years, counting from the day that the said Book shall be finished printing for the first time, under penalty against violators of three thousand livres <note>A significant monetary fine in 17th-century French currency.</note> in fines, confiscation of the counterfeit Copies, and of all dam-

the first, and perhaps the only one among those who have given them to us; as for example, it is very constant and certain that all our old Romances <note>The "Romances" here refer to medieval chivalric tales, such as those of Charlemagne.</note> took their origin from the Chronicles of Bishop Turpin; the tales of Pope Joan from a Marianus Scotus, the salvation of Trajan from a John the Levite, and the opinion that Virgil was a Magician from the monk Helinandus; and once that author is found, one must diligently consider his condition, the party he followed, and the time in which he first wrote: because one has much more assurance in those who have handled public affairs than in monks and private individuals, and in men of high and sublime status than in the simple and ignorant. The second [point], because all Historians, except those who are perfectly heroic, never represent things to us in their pure state, but incline and mask them according to the face they wish them to take; and to give credit to their judgment and attract others to it, they willingly lend weight to the matter from that side, lengthening and amplifying it, slanting and disguising it as they judge appropriate: from which we see that the Pagans <note>original: "Gentils"</note> and Idola-

### CHAPTER II.

#### *Concerning Magic, and its various types.*

<margin>Alciato, Emblem 187.</margin>
Since the famous Jurist <note>Andrea Alciato (1492–1550), an Italian lawyer and author of the highly influential "Emblemata."</note> took the opportunity to represent to us in his Emblems the three causes of ignorance under the image of the Sphinx—pleasure by its face, inconstancy by its feathers, and pride by its feet—I believe that one could not fail, in completing this portrait, to remark upon its effect through the cruelty of the same monster. For just as the Sphinx took pleasure in casting down from the height of its rock all those who could not or would not solve its riddles, so too has ignorance always applied itself to making those fall—and as it were, casting them down from their credit and reputation—who, having better occupations, did not wish to amuse themselves with such puerilities and trifles. Indeed, we see that before the Humanities <term>Humanities: the study of classical Greek and Latin literature and philosophy</term> and polite Letters had been made common and accessible to everyone through the felicity of our last century, all those who occupied themselves

### OF GREAT MEN.

Regarding the branches of **Natural Philosophy** <note>The precursor to modern science, focusing on the study of the physical world</note>, he attributes to it first **Medicine**, followed by **Chemistry**, **Astronomy**, **Physiognomy** <note>The study of character through facial features</note>, and **Oniroscopy** <note>The interpretation of dreams</note>; to which one must also add **Chiromancy** <note>Palm reading</note>, **Metoposcopy** <note>Reading character from the lines of the forehead</note>, **Helioscopy** <note>Observation of the sun</note>, and **Geomancy** <note>Divination by means of earth or geographic features</note>. Specifically, the first three should be related to Physiognomy, and the last one at least—as **Albert the Great**, **Vigenère**, **Fludd**, **Pomponazzi**, and **Agrippa** maintain—to **Astrology**. All of these branches, because they have their foundations in the causes of Nature, can be practiced freely (as these Authors say) and without suspicion of any Magic other than the **Natural Magic** permitted and approved by everyone. This is provided, however, that one remains as precisely as possible within the limits of their natural causes, without abandoning them to a thousand ridiculous observations which slip only too easily into the minds of those who practice them.

### CHAPTER III.

**That many great personages have been esteemed as Magicians, who were only Politicians.**

If it were permitted to add something to that observation worthy of consideration, upon which is built the first Chapter of the Essays of the "Seneca of France" <note>A reference to Michel de Montaigne (1533–1592), often called the French Seneca for his stoic leanings and essay style.</note>, that by diverse and entirely different means one can arrive at a similar end: I do not believe that one could choose any example more capable of verifying the truth of this maxim than that which presents itself in the punishment of lying and fabulous Authors. The malice of these authors could be repressed by a means entirely contrary to that which was anciently practiced by the Lycians <note>An ancient civilization in what is now Turkey.</note> against false witnesses and informers; for those people having the custom of treating them as slaves and of selling and delivering them in the public square; it would be necessary, on the contrary, to establish a law that all Histories should be similar to those contracts which are named by

<margin>
Heraclides in his fragments on politics. <note>original: "Heraclides infrag. de politiis." Referring to Heraclides Ponticus, a Greek philosopher.</note>
</margin>

# APOLOGY

After having long considered what could be the cause for so many people to comment so disadvantageously on the doctrine of all these great personages, I have first persuaded myself that it could be for a reason common to all false beliefs that slip insensibly among us; especially since, as the Chancellor of England <note>Francis Bacon (1561–1626), a foundational figure in the scientific revolution.</note> remarks, <margin>Book 3, The Great Instauration <note>original: "lib. 3. instur. mag." referring to Bacon's *Instauratio Magna*.</note></margin> *It is an error proper and perpetual to the human intellect, that it is more moved and excited by affirmatives than by negatives.*

Or perhaps it is because these Philosophers rose to contemplations so high and elevated above the common sort, that all those who did nothing but crawl by comparison were constrained to admire them; following which, they blamed them as being too audacious and supernatural. This was either because they judged them such through the weakness of their own judgment, or rather because they did so with the intent to slander them, since as Seneca <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca (c. 4 BC – AD 65), Roman Stoic philosopher.</note> says, <margin>On the Happy Life <note>original: "de vita beata."</note></margin> *as great as the crowd of admirers is, just as great is the crowd of the envious.*

This has brought such confusion to all things, and principally to the **sciences**, that if it is not now impossible, at least one must confess that it is greatly difficult to be able to discern the legitimate **Professors** of these from all the ignorant and reckless people who meddle in practicing them. Because they have muddled them with an infinity of frauds and superstitions, they have rendered these sciences so suspect that even those who have cultivated them most religiously have never been able to do so with the entire approbation and satisfaction of everyone. to explain: how it happens that many people, having seen in these catalogs that Alexander of Aphrodisias <note>A celebrated Aristotelian commentator from the 2nd century AD.</note> had written on the **magic arts**, Saint Thomas <note>Thomas Aquinas, the influential 13th-century theologian.</note> on **judicial astrology** <note>The practice of predicting human affairs and earthly events through the positions of celestial bodies.</note>, and Roger Bacon <note>A 13th-century English philosopher and friar often associated with early experimental science.</note> on **Necromancy** <note>The practice of communicating with the dead to predict the future.</note>, have imagined these writings to be the exact opposite of what one ought to judge of them. They believe that these works contain nothing other than the precepts and various methods one must follow to become instructed in the practice of all these divinations; and that, consequently, it was not without reason that their authors were held and reputed to be magicians.

This is, nevertheless, a conclusion so vain, slight, and ill-founded, that besides this first falsehood one may remark yet another. This second error, because it is not so manifest, has deceived many people to this day who have believed that it was enough to write on magic to be declared an Enchanter and a magician. # APOLOGY

Consider the doubtful treatises of Hippocrates and Galen <note>Famous ancient Greek physicians whose massive bodies of work contain many forgeries</note>, or those works called into question by Erasmus during the printing of the Church Fathers; consider the small booklets attributed to Gerson, Fenestella, Pythagoras, and Cato, and all those deemed suspect among the Humanists. # OF GREAT MEN.

Ammonius <note>Ammonius Hermiae (c. 440–517 AD), a Greek philosopher and commentator on Aristotle.</note> testifies in his Commentary on the *Categories*, that there were found in that sumptuous Library of the city of Alexandria forty books of the *Analytics* which all bore the name of Aristotle, even though he had only composed four of them, of which the first two correspond to the nine that are cited by Diogenes Laertius. This must be attributed, as Galen <note>Claudius Galenus (129–c. 216 AD), the prominent Greek physician and philosopher.</note> remarks, to the emulation <margin>*Commentary on the book of Hippocrates on human nature.*</margin> that existed between the Kings of Pergamon and Alexandria to well-reward those who brought them the books of some good Author, and principally of Aristotle, to further adorn their Library; it having never happened previously that the titles of ancient books had been falsified. We would deduce this more amply if it had not already been done by Patrizi <note>Francesco Patrizi (1529–1597), a Venetian philosopher and scientist who critiqued Aristotelianism.</note>, or if it <margin>*Peripatetic Discussions, volume 1, book 1.*</margin> were necessary, to verify that it is wrongly and without any appearance of reason that people circulate under the names of all those who have been in vogue, on account of their unparalleled doctrine, an infinity of disjointed fragments, poorly made rhapsodies, fabulous treatises, useless writings, and books composed without reason, method, or judgment,

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>The previous page likely discussed the superstitions of the common people and the role of the clergy in addressing them. The catchword "enuoyé" (sent) connects to the final sentence of this page.</meta>

# APOLOGY

It was a common opinion, held by many as true, that there were in his time certain **Enchanters** <note>The author refers to the era of Agobard, Archbishop of Lyon (9th century), who wrote against these superstitions.</note> who possessed the power to stir up hail, thunder, and tempests whenever they pleased, in order to spoil and destroy all the fruits of the earth. They would then sell these spoils to certain inhabitants of the land of **Magonia** <note>original: "Magodie"; a mythical cloud-realm mentioned in medieval folklore.</note> who arrived every year in **ships through the air** to restock themselves with these provisions. This was held to be so certain and assured that this good Bishop had great difficulty one day in rescuing three men and a woman from the hands of the foolish populace, who were dragging them to their execution under the belief that they had fallen from these ships.

Although this opinion destroys itself well enough by the lack of agreement among those who maintain it—and by the contradictions encountered therein, as is common with all other lies—it is nevertheless necessary, in order to uproot it totally and provide a remedy as powerful as this malady is deep-seated, to reduce all these opinions to four principal ones. The king of the Bactrians <note>A region in Central Asia, modern-day Afghanistan/Uzbekistan.</note> against whom Ninus <note>The mythical founder of Nineveh and the Assyrian Empire.</note> waged war was named Oxyartes. Neither Ninus nor Ctesias <note>A Greek physician and historian of the 5th century BCE.</note>—who, according to the report of Arnobius <note>An Early Christian apologist (c. 300 CE).</note>, wrote his history in great detail—make any mention of his Magic. He was a subject of Ninus, a contemporary of Abraham, and from the land of Chaldea <note>An ancient region in Mesopotamia.</note>. <margin>Book on the seven-month birth.</margin>
Since Hippocrate <note>Ancient Greek physician, often called the "Father of Medicine."</note> says that children, as soon as they are born, seem to laugh or cry while sleeping, and that while awake they also laugh and cry immediately of their own accord before forty days have passed: This could have happened particularly to Zoroaster <note>An ancient Persian prophet; the author is defending him against the charge of being a malevolent sorcerer.</note>, because of a great abundance of spirits <note>In early modern medicine, "spirits" were subtle fluids that animated the body.</note>, and consequently of heat, which, by delivering him from that humidity common to others, excited in him this action. This might well signify that he would one day be a great personage, but not a Magician: just as, in truth, this sign has always been esteemed so fortunate that it gave Virgil occasion to say in his Eclogues,

<margin>Eclogue 4.</margin>
> ——— *Those who have not smiled upon their parents,*
> *No god honors with his table, nor goddess with her bed.*
<note>original: "Qui non risere parentes, Nec deus hunc mensa, dea nec dignata cubili est."</note>

Because those who laugh so early are ordinarily more lively and cheerful, or as Hippocrates calls them, <term>Prothymoteres</term> <note>Greek: προθυμότεροι; meaning those who are more eager, courageous, or ready of heart.</note>, which is to say they have a prompt and skillful heart, and for this reason give more hope for their fortune than those who are mournful, slow, and of a dull and heavy spirit. <margin>Pliny, Book 7, Chapter 16.</margin>
A greater omen, according to what Pliny reports, was the movement of his brain; for it is the common condition of all newborn children to have a certain cavity at the part of the skull where the future <term>sagittal suture</term> <note>The seam running front-to-back along the top of the skull.</note> joins with the <term>coronal suture</term> <note>The seam running across the top of the skull from ear to ear.</note>, which is covered by a thick and dense membrane. At this spot—at least until it has been converted into bone—one can easily recognize *by sight and touch* <note>original: "vifu & tactu"</note> (as Mr. Riolan, a very learned anatomist, has remarked in his *Osteology*) the continual beating of the brain. This was perhaps noticed more strongly and vehemently in Zoroaster than it usually appears in many other children, because of that abundance of spirits and natural heat which we have shown to have been particular to him. Finally, if one wishes to infer that Zoroaster was a magician because Pliny says he remained twenty years in solitude, and because Suidas and Volaterran testify that he died being struck by lightning; it would be equally necessary to conclude that Epimenides, who remained there fifty years, that Moses, who passed the third part of his life there, and that all the Fathers of the—

### CHAPTER IX.

#### That Orpheus was not a Magician.

Since it is the scope and extent of human nature to judge spiritual things only by those that are sensible and material, substances only by their accidents <note>In philosophy, "accidents" are qualities that do not change the essence of a thing, such as color or size.</note>, and everything it wishes to know only by appearances; it seems to me that the one and only means remaining to us to disengage the truth from all these veils and coverings is to consider them as closely as possible, and never to use—when making their just estimation and sorting—that prejudiced opinion which often makes us choose and prefer shadows over bodies, darkness over light, and the most disordered fables over certain and true histories. This must be done with all the more diligence and circumspection in this Chapter, as there is nothing, according to Plutarch <note>A Greek-Roman historian and philosopher known for his "Parallel Lives" and essays on ethics.</note>,

# APOLOGY

To refute the arguments we are now considering, one must recall that according to the testimony of all the best authors, the <term>Bacchanalian or Dionysian Orgies</term><note>Ancient Greek festivals and rituals dedicated to Dionysus (Bacchus), the god of wine and ecstasy.</note> were first established by Orpheus in his homeland of Thrace. He ordained that they should be celebrated by women during the time of their <term>monthly purges</term><note>A historical term for menstruation.</note> in order to separate them from the company of their husbands during that period, and to prevent the accidents that can occur if they conceive in such a state. 

However, as he had recognized through experience that the women were ashamed to attend to these rites—because doing so revealed what they were accustomed to concealing with every kind of artifice—he realized he would be forced to abolish the rituals to his great dishonor unless he promptly provided a remedy. Taking the opportunity presented by this distaste, he decided to make the festivals more celebrated by permitting all women to practice them on certain days which he specifically designated for these ceremonies. This they did afterwards with such great liberty and rejoicing that, in addition to their dances which they timed to the sound of <term>drums and cymbals</term><note>Original: "tambours & cymbales." These instruments were central to the rhythmic, ecstatic nature of Dionysian worship.</note>, and the voices and acclamations they raised—

# APOLOGY

We can, I say, demonstrate in a few words that these Hymns contain nothing that should in any way make them suspected of Magic, whether one explains them precisely according to the letter, or whether one wishes to follow the various interpretations of their allegorical and moral sense. And that this is true of the first <note>The literal or "letter" of the text</note>, one can easily recognize it if one considers the skill of this first Theologian <note>Referring to Orpheus, traditionally considered the father of Greek theology</note>, who, in order to tame and polish the spirit of a rustic and coarse People, wished to make use of the strongest and most powerful means that one could ever have invented to happily achieve his undertaking. This was to instill in their minds the fear and respect of certain Divinities, whom he himself wished to celebrate in his Hymns, as much to give them popularity and credit by his example, as to leave a model for all his successors of the various ways of acting and ceremonies that had to be observed to maintain the honor and devotion of their sacrifices. These were truly diverse and entirely dissimilar, because, like all the ceremonies that we have

# APOLOGY

<margin>Chapter 14, Book of Natural Questions <note>original: "cap. 14 lib. nat. quæst." Referring to Seneca the Younger's work on the natural world.</note></margin> If we wish to interpret them in their true sense, it must be noted along with Seneca that the Egyptians—from whom this first Philosopher and Theologian <note>Likely referring to Orpheus or Hermes Trismegistus.</note> had drawn all his doctrine—divided each Element into two parts, one of which they called the male, and the other the female. For example, in the Earth, the rocks and pebbles; in the Water, the sea; in the Air, the winds; and in the Fire, the flame and the thunder, held the place of the strongest and most robust Element. Conversely, the soft and manageable Earth, the fresh Water, the tranquil Air, and the Fire which is of almost no activity, held the place of the weakest and most feeble. This later gave cause to our Orpheus to similarly place two distinct and different virtues in all the bodies of this universe, one of which was solely intended to govern its Sphere, and the other to produce the effects which depended upon its perfection. This is why, wishing to let this doctrine flow with the sweetness of his Hymns, he composed them all under the name of each of these virtues, calling those he gave to the Earth Pluto and Proserpine; to the Water, Thetis and Ocean; to the Air, Jupiter and Juno; to the Fire, Aurora and Phanes <note>original: "Phanete." Phanes is a primary Orphic deity of light and generation.</note>: and giving the name of each...

If we were not taught by Plutarch regarding the saying of Pythagoras—who was accustomed to freely confess and acknowledge that the greatest fruit he had ever gathered from Philosophy was to be astonished by nothing at all—I could hardly persuade myself that he would not be greatly amazed now, were he to consider how the malice and ignorance of men has so changed the truth of his history and the true sense of his doctrine. Just as also in one of his Symbols <note>The "Pythagorean Symbols" were cryptic maxims or proverbs used by his followers</note> he expressly forbade the use of beans, which, following the same superstition, he would boil and expose for several nights to the Moon, until by some great feat of Magic they came to be converted into blood. the tale of Aristophanes in the comedy *The Clouds*, where they say that this philosopher <note>Pythagoras</note> wrote whatever he pleased with blood upon a convex mirror, and that by holding these letters up to the face of the Moon when it was full, he saw in the orb of that celestial body everything he had written on the surface of his mirror. To this one may also add that he appeared with a golden thigh at the Olympic Games; that he was greeted by the river Nessus; that he stopped the flight of an eagle, tamed a bear, caused a serpent to die, and drove away an ox that was spoiling a field of beans, solely by the power of certain words. And furthermore, that he was seen on the same day and at the same hour in the city of Crotone and in that of Metapontum, and that he predicted future events with such certainty that many maintain he was named Pythagoras because he gave answers no less certain and true than those of the Pythian Apollo <note>The oracle at Delphi</note>. This he was able to do through <term>Onomancy</term> <note>Divination based on the meaning or numerical value of a person's name</note>, which was very familiar to him, as is easy to judge by the fragments remaining to us of his superstitious arithmetic and of the wheel attributed to him by Fludd <note>Robert Fludd, an English physician and occultist</note> and Cataneo <note>Girolamo Cataneo, an Italian mathematician</note>. I am ashamed

For my part, I believe he would be equally agitated by two different passions; and that if he did not marvel at the lack of judgment in those who say things about him that they would be scrupulous about asserting even of the most notorious <term>juggler</term> <note>original: "basteleur," a street performer or magician of low status</note> and <term>charlatan</term> <note>original: "charlatant," a traveling quack or fraud</note> who has ever lived, at the very least he would have compassion for the fact that they bring so little judgment to the selection and sorting of all these proofs, which cannot in any way be received as legitimate. For we can say generally of these things, that there is no appearance of belief that a man who was so serious throughout his life, and so learned (as we have shown above), would have wished to amuse himself with all these vain follies and subtleties, which never had any other occasion for their first birth and origin...

# APOLOGY

In the year that Publius Cornelius and Marcus Bebius were Consuls <note>181 BCE</note>, there is no doubt that one must grant—following all the <term>Demonographers</term> <note>Writers who study or categorize demons and magic</note>, and principally **le Loyer** and **Delrio** <note>Pierre le Loyer and Martin Delrio, influential 16th-century authorities on witchcraft and the supernatural</note> who are the most learned among them—that **Numa Pompilius** was truly the greatest Sorcerer and Magician of all those who have ever worn a Crown. They claim he had even more power over Devils than over men, since he made use of the industry of the former to render the Romans more flexible and compliant in the execution of his laws and commands.

But if we wish to show how all these Authors abuse too freely their leisure and ours, by conceiving ideas and forms so hideous and so strange only to hatch them with great difficulty—wanting no less to arrest our eyes than to engage and enslave our belief—it is only necessary to see and contemplate the first portrait of this personage. This is found not only in **Livy** and **Dionysius of Halicarnassus** <note>Ancient historians who recorded the early legends of Rome</note>, who traced the first and roughest sketches of him, but particularly in **Plutarch**, who dressed him in his true colors and in all the

Egeria <note>A water nymph in Roman mythology said to have been the wife and advisor of Numa Pompilius.</note> was nothing but a pure pretense and stratagem of that cunning politician, who wished to establish the authority of his laws, sacrifices, and constitutions through this fable; as the same Lactantius <note>Lactantius (c. 250 – c. 325), an early Christian author who advised Constantine I.</note> very well remarked when he said, speaking again of Numa, that to establish these things *with some authority, he feigned that he had nightly meetings with the Goddess Egeria.* <note>original Latin: "aliqua cum authoritate, simulauit cum Dea Ægeria nocturnos se habere congressus."</note> This has many times made me marvel at the lethargic and drowsy judgment, or the lack of conscience, shown by our Demonographers <note>Writers who studied or documented beliefs in demons and magic, often used here as a pejorative for those who believe in superstitions.</note> in so freely depraving the authority of this author and that of Halicarnassus, Plutarch, and Livy, in order to establish and give some luster and color to what they want us to believe, and to found the truth of their proposition upon the most manifest falsehood imaginable. For if one wishes to believe Le Loyer and Delrio <note>Pierre Le Loyer and Martin Delrio were prominent 16th-century writers on demonology and the occult.</note>, the principal authors <margin>Book 2, chapter 5</margin> who maintain all the fables we have told of Numa are Plutarch and Dionysius of Halicarnassus; yet if we come to read and leaf through them, we shall find quite the contrary: that it is they who refute them, who undermine and expose them, and who warn us not to place any faith in them.

And to prove this point, beginning with the opinion they held regarding his Nymph Egeria, Plutarch <note>Greek biographer and essayist, c. 46 – 119 AD</note>, after having long discoursed upon the probability of believing in these divine apparitions, finally concludes what his own opinion was with these words: [ Nevertheless, if there is anyone of another mind, the path is wide and open; for I myself do not find it without merit what others reveal concerning Lycurgus <note>The legendary lawgiver of Sparta</note> and Numa <note>Numa Pompilius, the second king of Rome</note>, and other similar personages, who, having to manage rude and fierce peoples, and wishing to introduce great innovations into the governments of their countries, wisely feigned to have communication with the Gods, seeing that this fiction was useful and salutary even to those whom they made believe it. ] Which he confirms anew when he says three or four pages below, immediately after having cited the verses of Timon the Phliasian <note>A skeptical philosopher and poet</note>, that the pretense with which Numa clothed himself was the love of a Goddess, or perhaps of a Mountain Nymph, and the secret interviews he feigned to have with her. ### CHAPTER XII.

#### Concerning Democritus, Empedocles, and Apollonius.

I would never have been so bold as to displace the precious and venerable boundaries of Antiquity—which the God Terminus <note>The Roman deity who presided over boundary markers; his statues were never to be moved.</note> in the fabulous Theology of the Romans signified to us ought to be as if immovable—if I had not based my reasoning on the fact that Antiquity is called in Arnobius <note>Arnobius of Sicca, an Early Christian apologist.</note> *the mother most full of errors*, <margin>Book 1.</margin> <note>original: "errorum plenissima mater"</note> in order to judge that it was no sacrilege at all to call into doubt what has been held as true. This is especially so after so many centuries which, in their long and variable revolutions, are accustomed—both in civil and natural history—to drag after them a long tail of fables, and to give them new strength and growth from day to day through the great number of those who allow themselves to be deceived <note>The original French "pipper" refers to being duped or caught like a bird in a snare.</note> by respect for Antiquity's great old age. Likewise, it would be too great a severity to wish to force us to follow the superstitious routine of those who dare not touch this troubled Antiquity, the—

*Lest he should seem so to his citizens.*
For beyond the lack of probability and the diversity of these reasons, one would have to contradict Hippocrates <note>The famous Greek physician, often called the father of medicine.</note> in his Epistle to Damagetus, where he says that, having been called by the people of Abdera to remedy the madness of Democritus, he found him occupied with the reading of certain books and the dissection of several animals—actions which are certainly far removed from those who have lost their sight. Since it is certain that his laughter was a moral judgment, it is more appropriate to believe that his blindness was likewise metaphorical; and that the fables of Antiquity, following the opinion of Scaliger <note>Julius Caesar Scaliger, a renowned Renaissance scholar.</note>, represented him as blind *because he did not use his eyes in the manner of the common people.* I similarly estimate that there is no appearance of truth in what is said of Empedocles: that he threw himself into the gorges and flames of Mount Etna <note>original: "mont Gibel," a historical name for Mount Etna derived from the Arabic 'Jebel'.</note>, *so that when he suddenly disappeared,* as Lactantius <note>An early Christian author and advisor to Constantine.</note> says, *it might be believed he had departed to the Gods.* For Empedocles was so far from having such high and lofty ambition that, on the contrary, Diogenes Laërtius <note>A biographer of the Greek philosophers.</note> testifies that he refused, with incredible constancy, the Royal Crown that was presented to him—

# APOLOGY

I find only two things among these objections from Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, Roman author of Natural History</note> that can give us any pause: namely, the books on Magic that Democritus composed, and those of Dardanus which he brought to light. To which, nevertheless, one can respond in a few words that such proofs do not conclude directly—as we have shown in the sixth Chapter of this Apology—that these books are not specified by Laërtius <note>Diogenes Laërtius, biographer of Greek philosophers</note> nor any other, and that it is greatly uncertain who this Dardanus might have been. For even though Pliny, Tertullian, and Apuleius pass him off as a great Magician, they nevertheless speak of him only upon the authority of Columella, who says in his tenth book:

> *But if no medicine has the power to drive away the plague,*
> *let the arts of Dardanus be summoned.* <note>original Latin: "At si nulla valet medicina depellere pestem, Dardaniæ veniant artes."</note> -----

And if one refers to the Legal Scholars, this Dardanus could well have been something other than an Enchanter, since they say that *Dardanarii* <note>monopolists or forestallers</note> are properly *Seplasiarii* <note>perfumers or ointment-sellers</note>, *Propolae* <note>retailers</note>, and *Proxenatae* <note>brokers</note>; that is to say, brokers and resellers who fill their granaries and warehouses with all sorts of provisions in order to sell them very dearly when there is a scarcity.

He rejected all other kinds of Demons and Angels in order to remain firm in his principles, refusing to admit anything that was not known to him either through motion or through operation. All these natural truths which he says were known to him are today rendered greatly suspicious and doubtful by a swarm of innovators which grows from day to day under the leadership of Telesio, Patrizzi, Campanella, Verulam <note>Francis Bacon, Baron Verulam</note>, Giordano Bruno, and Basson <note>Sebastien Basson, an early anti-Aristotelian atomist</note>, who truly have no other design than to elbow out this Philosophy, and ruin this great building that Aristotle and more than twelve thousand who have interpreted him have endeavored to build through such a long succession of years. # APOLOGY

Regarding de Moura <note>likely Manuel de Moura, a 17th-century commentator</note>, one can say that he clearly misrepresents Philoponus <note>John Philoponus, a 6th-century Christian philosopher and commentator on Aristotle</note>, who says nothing else—following the Greek text and the old translation consistent with that of Nunnesius <note>Pedro Juan Nuñez, a Spanish humanist</note>—except that Aristotle, having reached the age of seventeen, was advised by the Pythian Oracle to devote himself principally to Philosophy. The article of his will by which he commanded that statues be made which he had vowed for Nicanor <note>Aristotle's adopted son and executor</note>, would serve in a pinch as a more certain proof than the preceding ones, if this shrewd Philosopher had not practiced such a ruse, in imitation of Socrates, to prevent his memory from being defamed by the suspicion of Atheism, and to leave a perpetual *synderesis* <term>synderesis: the innate moral consciousness or "spark" of conscience</term> and remorse of conscience to those who had accused him of it. This could justify him much better than the three sacrifices he made to the Gods, or the knowledge of the Trinity that many Catholic Doctors have attributed to him: for these are all chimeras <note>fanciful illusions or unfounded ideas</note> which took their origin and foundation from what he says in his first book *On the Heavens* speaking of the Ternary number <note>the number three</note>: "Wherefore, having received from nature as it were her laws, we use this number even in the [sacrifices to the gods]" <note>original Greek: Διὸ παρὰ τῆς φύσεως εἰληφότες ὥσπερ νόμους ἐκείνης, καὶ πρὸς τὰς...</note>

<margin>Book 2, chapter 3.</margin>
<term>Astrologers</term> <note>Those who study the influence of stars on human affairs</note>, <term>Chemists</term> <note>Alchemists seeking to transform matter</note>, and Magicians would willingly tear them to pieces in order to claim for themselves the largest and best portion of each of them. It is, nevertheless, just as easy to judge by the fragments that remain of their works and compositions that they were Physicians, as it is entirely impossible to accurately prescribe and define all the particulars of their lives and the time of their birth. This is certainly as little known to us as that of the peoples who were called <term>Aborigines</term> <note>original: "Aborigenes"; referring to those believed to be the original inhabitants of a land from the beginning</note> and without origin, or those whom the Poets have described as descending from the clouds so as not to diminish the glory of their noble and generous actions by the lowliness of their beginnings. This should not be attributed so much to the lack of care the <term>Arabs</term> <note>referring here to the Islamic Golden Age scholars who preserved much Greek knowledge</note> had in leaving us some knowledge of them, as to the barbarism that reigned in their time among the <term>Latins</term> <note>Western Europeans using Latin as a scholarly language</note>. Furthermore, he had previously stated in his *Catalogue of Ecclesiastical Writers* that he did not hold as true what was said regarding the magic of Peter of Abano, because he had never noticed that he had written any book on that subject. To this, one might also add the silence of all the librarians and the confirmation that Symphorien Champier <note>A French physician and humanist (c. 1470–1539).</note> gives to this authority of Trithemius, when he asserts that he has never seen any of his books on magic, except <margin>Treatise on famous [men]</margin>

often encountered by those who leaf through his books—such as, for example, *Ens Pagoycum* <note>A Paracelsian term referring to the "ens" or essence of the body influenced by the stars</note>, *Cagastricum* <note>Diseases arising from the "cagaster," or corrupt seeds of nature</note>, *Cherionium* <note>A term for an incurable or malignant ulcer</note>, *Leffas lesadach* <note>Paracelsian terms for the vital spirits of the earth and trees</note>, *Trarames*, *Stannar*, *Perenda*, *Relloleum* <note>Various neologisms coined by Paracelsus to describe occult properties of matter</note>: and an infinity of other similar words, which leave the reader so doubtful and uncertain of what he means to say, that he walks only by groping through such Meanders <note>A reference to the winding river Meander, signifying a labyrinthine path</note>. He would be unable to discern when the author speaks of a clod of earth or a pill, of a stone or a loaf of bread, of the Devil or of Nature; with even greater reason might he doubt whether the author uses Magic as Enigmas (following the example of Trithemius <note>Johannes Trithemius, a German abbot and occultist known for steganography or hidden writing</note>) to veil his precepts, and to avoid revealing the vanity of his Art, which he judged ought to be all the more admired the less it was understood.

> *For fools admire and love all things more*
> *Which they perceive hidden under inverted words.*
<note>original Latin: "Omnia enim stolidi magis admirantur amantique / Inuersis quæ sub verbis latitantia cernunt."</note>

<margin>Lucretius, Book 1.</margin>

<meta>This page continues the defense of Paracelsus against accusations of sorcery, focusing on the interpretation of his "magical" terminology as a coded language for chemical processes.</meta>

# APOLOGY

<margin>In a Letter written to Paracelsus.</margin>
The question of his Magic involves the opinion of his principal interpreters, Severinus the Dane <note>Petrus Severinus (1541–1602), a Danish physician who codified Paracelsian theory.</note> and Crollius <note>Oswald Croll (1563–1609), an alchemist and professor of medicine.</note>, who make it serve only as a veil and cover for his doctrine. This is witnessed by what the latter says on page 77 of his Preface: *that Paracelsus wrote in a magical style for the experts, not for the common people, but for himself and for the wise sons of wisdom educated in the magical school, hiding his mysteries under various names* <note>original: "Paracelsum expertes stilo magico scripsisse, non vulgo, sed sibi et intelligentibus in schola magica educatis sapientiæ filijs, mysteria sua sub varijs nominibus occultasse"</note>. Indeed, it is certain that the names of many spirits which he very often intermingles in his books—and which one might mistake for lesser demons—must be interpreted according to the opinion of Jacques Gohory <note>A French scholar (1520–1576) who introduced Paracelsian alchemy to France.</note>, who was the first promoter of Paracelsianism in France. These names refer to extracts and various essences, their properties and preparations, or finally to mineral, vegetable, and animal substances which he used for the composition of his remedies. Furthermore, it is true that Johannes Oporinus <note>A famous Swiss printer and former secretary to Paracelsus.</note>, who was his servant for a long time and who seems to have been the first to reveal everything that is now objected against him, makes no mention of his Magic nor of his invocations; and Wetterus, who lived with him for 27 months, says nothing else of it, except

<margin>Commentary on book 4 of Paracelsus's "On Long Life."</margin>
<margin>found in Erasmus, part 1.</margin>

<meta>catchword: except</meta>

<summary>This page argues that the "magical" language used by Paracelsus was actually a coded chemical shorthand intended for initiates, rather than evidence of demonic sorcery. It cites early Paracelsian scholars and personal associates like Oporinus to support the idea that his "spirits" were merely metaphors for medicinal essences.</summary>

<keywords>Paracelsus, Magic, Alchemy, Petrus Severinus, Oswald Croll, Jacques Gohory, Johannes Oporinus, Chemical Essences, Medical Terminology, Defense of Paracelsus</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the various categories of Magic as defined by certain practitioners, likely following a list of definitions from the preceding page.</meta>

->APOLOGIE<-

the meaning of signs that occur outside the order of nature, such as the Star <note>original: "l'Estoille"</note> which appeared to the Magi; the second concerns the metamorphosis and transmutation of bodies; the third, the power of words and speech; the fourth, rings and <term>gamahées</term> <note>Engraved stones or talismans believed to possess natural or supernatural powers</note>; the fifth, bewitched images; and the last, the <term>cabala</term> <note>Here referring to a form of practical magic or mystical tradition</note> which he claimed was occupied with performing all extraordinary actions that cannot be reduced to any of the other five parts. Such actions include making fruits ripen in an instant, making a horse travel further in one day than another would in a month, speaking intelligibly with those who are more than two hundred leagues distant from us; and, in short, doing everything that seems—and has always been held to be—impossible. 

But I am greatly astonished, seeing that he boasts of having had knowledge of all these types of Magic, why he never wished to perform anything by their means. It seems it would have been more appropriate to confirm this new doctrine by some of his own experiments, rather than following the ordinary path of <term>charlatans</term> <note>Street performers or quacks who sold medicines and performed tricks</note>, who deploy a torrent of common and popular eloquence.

<summary>The author critiques a practitioner of magic who claims mastery over six supernatural categories—including talismans and long-distance communication—yet fails to provide any experimental proof of his abilities.</summary>

I do not wish to deny, however, that the opinion of those others is even more acceptable, who say that one of the principal advantages that learned and industrious men have over the ignorant is the ability to establish new systems and principles, and to change the order, the precepts, and the method of the Sciences, lengthening or shortening them at their whim like a stirrup strap. They say that Paracelsus <note>Theophrastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist who famously challenged traditional medical authorities.</note>, being one of those men, wished to change the face of Magic just as he had done for Medicine and Philosophy. He even boasted of being able to do the same for Religion, threatening the Pope and Luther <note>Martin Luther, the leader of the Protestant Reformation.</note> that he would bring them both in line with his maxims whenever he had the will to do so.
